This he said, so that he might learn where he was, and in order to send and kill him. Then, having called the scribes, he asked where the Scripture says Christ was to be born. They said, "In Bethlehem of Judea." Then he sent out his soldiers to kill all the children who were in Bethlehem, from two years old and under, according to the time which he had ascertained from the Magi. For this reason, an angel sent from God said to Joseph, "Arise, and take the child and his mother, and flee into Egypt." And Joseph did so, and having taken the child and his mother, he departed into Egypt.
ON THE SAME MONTH. The twenty-sixth day.
The Theotokos flees into Egypt.
The Theotokos flees into Egypt with the infant and Joseph for two reasons: that what was said by the prophet might be fulfilled, "Out of Egypt I called my Son," and that every mouth of the heretics might be stopped. For if the infant had not fled, but had been captured and slain, surely no place would have been left for the salvation of men. But if He could not have been destroyed by the sword or by any other kind of punishment, so as to fulfill the dispensation, many would have thought that He had assumed human flesh phantastically and not truly. "If He had flesh," they say, "He would certainly have been cut to pieces by the sword"; since without any pretext the wretched heretics dared to say that He was born phantastically. For this reason He flees into Egypt, so that He might crush the idols there and bring salvation to the whole world, according to the time when He was to be affixed to the cross and to rise again.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
The twenty-seventh day.
The Passion of the holy and glorious Protomartyr and Archdeacon Stephen
Stephen the protomartyr became a disciple of the holy apostles; and after the ascension of our Lord Jesus Christ, he was ordained with six others to minister to the faithful at the tables. D Being full of faith and wisdom, he performed great wonders and signs in
the people, teaching them and evangelizing concerning Christ. Then a dispute arising from the Libertines and Cyrenaeans, they stood up against Stephen and disputed with him; and being defeated, they became enraged because the holy Stephen had rebuked them and convicted them by bringing forth testimonies from the Holy Scriptures. And gnashing their teeth at him, they cast stones. But he, while being stoned, went upon his knees and prayed, saying: "Lord, do not lay this sin to their charge." And having said this, he saw the heavens opened, and the Son of God standing at the right hand of the power of the Father, and calling him; and thus he was perfected.
THE SAME MONTH, THE TWENTY-EIGHTH DAY
B 61 The contest of our holy Father Theodore the Graptos (The Inscribed).
He was from Syria, together with his brother Theophanes, the writer of canons. Having first embraced the monastic life in the Laura of Saint Sabas, and having struggled much toward virtue, he later came to Constantinople during the reign of Theophilus the Iconoclast. And being pressured to deny the veneration of the holy icons, and not being persuaded, he was sent into exile, having first been pierced with needles and his honorable face inscribed with needles and ink, and having been insulted like one of the criminals and runaway slaves; because he spoke boldly for the sake of justice, and did not wish to deny the honor and reverence of the holy and venerable icons, but remained to the end for the sake of the true confession. Having departed into exile, and having once again endured many struggles and strengthened many of the orthodox, he migrated to the Lord.
On the same day.
62 Commemoration of our holy Father Theodore, Archbishop of Constantinople.
This man was both born and bred in the same Constantinople. He became first, on account of his piety, a presbyter of the most holy Great Church; and not only a presbyter, but also a syncellus and skeuvophylax. For he was held in such great honor by the emperors, the patriarch, the senate, and the entire synod: for he was prudent, wise, almsgiving, compassionate, and carrying within himself all things that please God. Wherefore, the patriarch Constantine before him having died, by the vote of the emperor, the senate, the bishops, and all the people, he was chosen patriarch of the throne of Constantinople. And having shepherded his flock well, and having governed the holy Church in an orthodox and God-pleasing manner for two years and three months, he migrated to the Lord.
On the same day.
63 The contest of the holy martyrs Indes and Domna.
The martyr Domna was under the emperor Maximian in the city of Nicomedia, a priest of the twelve gods in the
palace. But having chanced upon the Acts of the holy Apostles and the Epistles of the holy Paul, and having had her soul illuminated by reading them, and having learned the true faith, she believed in Christ; and was baptized by Cyril, the bishop of Nicomedia, along with the eunuch Indes and many others. And from that time on, whatever she received from the palace, she began to distribute to the poor. When the chief of the eunuchs learned of this and was preparing to torture her, she feigned madness. For this reason, that she might be healed, she was sent to the bishop, and she lived among the Christians. But Maximian, having returned from the campaign, asked for Domna. And when he could not find her, he was seized with anger and ordered all Christians to be put to death. Then the holy Domna, having put on the habit of a man, would take up by night the relics of the saints and bury them. And having been recognized, she was beheaded.
On the same day.
The contest of the holy twenty thousand martyrs.
Maximian the emperor, having successfully concluded the war he had waged against the Ethiopians, and wishing to celebrate a victory in honor of the idols, issued a decree for everyone from everywhere to assemble to worship the idols. When, however, on the holy festival of the Nativity of Christ, the church of the Christians was full of people, and the Christians were celebrating the feast day with the greatest joy, they could not be induced to leave the church and worship impure demons. When Maximian learned of this, he ordered that brushwood be placed all around the church and set on fire, and that the Christians should be burned. Then indeed the bishop baptized all the catechumens, and having performed the sacred rites, he offered the most pure mysteries to all; and thus, when the fire was applied, twenty thousand people were consumed—except for the bishop, who was preserved by the grace of God so that he might help others and baptize them.
On the same day.
The contest of the holy martyrs who survived from the gathering and the pyre.
When the impure Maximian had done these things, he commanded that those Christians also who remained outside the pyre should be put to death. Therefore, the holy Indes, and Gorgonius, and Peter, with stones tied to their necks, were cast into the sea. Zeno, the commander of the troops, after his eyes and teeth had been crushed with a stone, was beheaded along with the chamberlain Dorotheus. Mardonius was consumed by fire; Mygdonius was cast into a pit; Glycerius the presbyter was delivered to the flames; Theophilus the deacon, after his tongue had been cut out, was stoned in the field; and many others on that same day received the crown of martyrdom. The holy Domna gathered the sacred relics of all these and buried them; afterwards, she placed them in a boat and hid them in a respectable place. Wherefore, having been reported to Maximian, by his order she was first beheaded, and then immediately burned with fire.
OF THE SAME MONTH. 29
The Massacre of the Holy Infants of Bethlehem.
When Herod, king of the Jews, had commanded the Magi to return and report to him concerning the newborn King, whom the star they were following had pointed out, so that he भी too might worship Him: and since the angel had told them not to return to Herod, but to depart into their own country by another way; and they having done this, Herod, seeing that he had been mocked by them, flew into a rage. Diligently inquiring about the time when the star appeared, he sent soldiers and slaughtered all the children in Bethlehem from two years old and under, with the intention that, by the children being destroyed, He who was to reign might also be extinguished, and not plot against him. But the madman labored in vain, not knowing that the divine counsel cannot be thwarted by man. Wherefore he secured for them the kingdom of heaven, but for himself, eternal punishment.
On the same day.
Commemoration of our Holy Father Marcellus, Abbot of the Monastery of the Unsleeping Ones.
This our holy Father Marcellus was from the city of Apamea in Syria, of a noble and illustrious family. He learned all knowledge and science. Then he renounced the world and his father’s house, and arrived in Ephesus. There, entering a monastery, he lived as a monk in a worthy manner. Afterwards, he came to the holy Alexander, who was in the monastery of the Unsleeping Ones, having learned of his godly and lofty virtue. And having lived in that monastery and surpassed all in virtue, after the death of Alexander and of Jacob, who had governed after Alexander, he became abbot. He contended so much in virtue that he received from God the gift of performing miracles and also of prophecy. Wherefore, having displayed the power of many wonders, and being honorable to emperors and all the people, he passed away in peace.
OF THE SAME MONTH. 30
Contest of the Holy Martyr Anysia.
The holy martyr Anysia lived under the Emperor Maximian, and was from the city of Thessalonica, having pious, faithful, and wealthy parents. But after they died, the holy one was left alone, continuing throughout her life and conduct to give thanks to God. One day, while she was going to church according to her custom, a soldier who was a worshiper of idols met her. Seizing her, he compelled her to go and sacrifice to the idols. But when he could not persuade the holy one, who resisted strongly and spat in his face, he became enraged, and ran his sword through her side. The holy one, having received this painful and bitter wound, fell immediately to the ground. But even having fallen, she did not cease from her thanksgiving and prayer to God. And thus, doing so for several hours, she surrendered her spirit to the Lord.
Eodem die. 69 The contest of the holy apostle Timon, one of the seven deacons.
Timon, the apostle of Christ, was one of the seventy disciples. He was ordained deacon by the holy apostles to serve the tables of those who came to Christ and were baptized, along with the other six deacons, among whom was the great Stephen, the protomartyr. But later, he was created bishop of Bostra in Arabia by the same holy apostles. As he taught in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, the true God, and persuaded many of the Greeks, both by his words and by miracles, to forsake the vain superstition of idols handed down to them by their ancestors and to embrace the true faith, he was sought after by the snares of the Jews, who could not bear that the name of Christ should be so freely preached. He was seized by impious idolaters and tortured with various torments; soon he was cast into a furnace, from whence, escaping unharmed and surviving for a while longer, he departed joyfully to Christ.
EODEM MENSE. Die tricesima prima. 71 Commemoration of Saint Melania the Roman.
When Theodosius the Great had exchanged his earthly kingdom for a heavenly one, and had departed to the immortal King, Arcadius, his eldest son, ruled over the New Rome and the territories subject to it, by his command; while Honorius, the younger son, held the empire of Old Rome and the regions subject to it. Under these emperors, Saint Melania flourished in the city of Rome, distinguished in nobility of birth, wealth, and beauty; and for this reason she was sought in marriage by many, although she herself abhorred weddings. When her parents had betrothed her against her will to a certain man, even before they came together, she instructed her spouse in the fear of God and succeeded so well in her speaking that he, despising the world and its pleasures, became a monk; she herself, having distributed to the poor an immense abundance of wealth which she had received from her parents, embraced the monastic life. And so she fought the good fight, that she even performed miracles. Finally, having built a monastery for the singing of praises to God, she died.
MENSE JANUARIO. Die prima. 73 Commemoration of the holy Circumcision of our Lord and God Jesus Christ.
Our God and Lord, to whom the hearts of men are open, and who foreknows future things, wishing to establish the faith of his Incarnation and dispensation, lest any of the heretics should be able to say that he was born phantastically and did not truly assume our flesh—as many of the heretics have dared to assert—and also to remove every pretext of caviling from the Jews,
so that they might not say that he turned aside from their custom and institutions; he fulfilled the entire law. And after eight days, from when he was immaculately born of the Virgin, he desired, as an infant, to be brought to that place where the Jews were accustomed to be circumcised: and he was circumcised, and his name was called Jesus, as it had been called by the angel before he was conceived in the incorrupt womb of the Theotokos. And having returned with his parents, he was brought up as is fitting for a human, progressing in wisdom, stature, and grace.
On the same day,
Commemoration of our holy Father Basil, Archbishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia.
Our holy Father Basil flourished under the Emperor Valens; and being outspoken, he reproved this very Valens, who was suffering from the Arian heresy, and almost induced him to renounce the heresy, had he not been ashamed to recant. But even if he did not entirely renounce his error, he nonetheless attended the church while Basil the Great was celebrating the Liturgy and offering gifts. Now Basil the Great was the son of Basil of Pontus and Emmelia of Cappadocia, a man so instructed in wisdom and knowledge that he easily surpassed all the ancients and the moderns; and he excelled so much in piety that he was made archbishop of the throne of Caesarea, where, sustaining many struggles for the orthodox faith, he stupefied emperors and prefects, and stopped the mouths of the heretics with his words. Finally, after he had composed many books and performed innumerable wonders, he migrated in peace to the Lord.
On the same day,
The struggle of the holy martyrs Theopemptus and Theodotus.
Missing.
IN THE SAME MONTH
The second day.
The struggle of the holy martyr Basil.
The martyr Basil lived under the reign of Julian the Apostate; he was from Ancyra in Galatia. But because he worshipped and confessed Christ, he was seized and brought before the governor Saturninus. And again, being asked before him if he believed in Christ, he cried out with a loud voice that there was no other God besides Him. For this reason he was suspended and most cruelly flayed with iron claws. Then, in bonds, he was sent from the city of Ancyra to Constantinople; where, being tortured by the prefect with most grievous torments, he was cast into a furnace of fire. But escaping from it unharmed, he was brought to Caesarea, to fight with beasts. And so, he was led, bound, into the middle of the theater on the day when the circus games were being celebrated. Then a ferocious lioness, first finished with hunger, was released to devour him. But the holy man, seeing the beast rushing upon him, began to pray; and while he prayed, he was, by the permission of God, devoured by the lioness, and brought his life to an end.
On the same day, a commemoration of our holy Father Silvester, Pope of Rome.
Silvester, illustrious for his wisdom and virtue, after the death of Miltiades, the bishop, was appointed bishop of old Rome. Having undertaken the governance of the Church, and feeding the great and sacred flock of the Christians, he underwent many struggles and performed very many miracles. He brought Constantine the Great, who was the first of the emperors to be a Christian, to the faith of Christ, and by holy baptism cleansed the leprosy of his body and, at the same time, the stains of his soul; for the holy Apostles Peter and Paul had appeared to him. It was he who was the author of the plan that, once the shrines of the idols and demons had been torn down, temples should be built in the name of Christ our God, of the Theotokos, of the holy apostles, and of the martyrs. He followed the emperor even to Byzantium, and instructed many in the doctrine of salvation. And thus, in deep old age, he joyfully departed to the Lord.
IN THE SAME MONTH. The third day.
The contest of the holy martyr Gordius.
Under the Emperor Licinius, the holy Gordius flourished, born in Caesarea in Cappadocia, of the rank of count, a centurion of soldiers. But since he could by no means endure the license and blasphemies against Christ of the impious Greeks, he resigned his military office and the fleeting and vanishing glory of this world, and withdrew to a mountain and dwelt in the wilderness; where, living among wild beasts, he gave himself up solely to prayer to God. But, vehemently inflamed with the desire for Christ and caught up by a certain impetus against superstition, he said to himself: "I will go into the city, and I will undergo martyrdom for the name of my Christ, that I may attain eternal life." Therefore, when the circus games were being held at Caesarea in the presence of a great multitude of people, he went out into the midst, and with a loud voice said: "I, who was not sought, have come of my own accord, and I profess myself a Christian, and I detest the lifeless idols, which I have loathed since my childhood." Moved by these words, the prefect ordered him to be beheaded.
On the same day, a commemoration of the holy and glorious prophet Malachi.
Malachi the prophet, after the people of the Jews had returned from captivity, was born in the region of Judea, and was of the tribe of Levi. While he was still a youth, he maintained that manner of life and rule which were pleasing to God; wherefore he was called "Angel" by the Jews: for he was of becoming appearance. But also, whatever he predicted was immediately confirmed by an angel and shown to be true; for the angel spoke with him, and many heard his voice when they encountered the prophet; however, it only happened to those who were worthy: for sinners could neither see him
neither did they see him, nor did they hear his voice. But at that time the prophet prophesied, in the days of anarchy, as it is written in the book of Judges; and having prophesied much, he died and was buried with his fathers in the field. He was, as we have said, comely, having a round face and a broad head.
ON THE SAME MONTH. The Contest of the Holy Hieromartyr Theogenes, Bishop of Parium.
Theogenes the hieromartyr was bishop of Parium; having been seized by the governor of the region, he was forced to serve in the army. But he declared himself to be a Christian, and a servant and soldier of Christ. Therefore, having been bound to four stakes by his hands and his feet, he was beaten by eight executioners with harsh rods. As one called Optio was passing by and insulting him, the saint prophesied concerning him that he would soon meet with destruction; which also came to pass. In the night, when the Lord appeared to the saint, He commanded him not to take or eat food from the hand of the Greeks. And he was chanting with the angels in the prison. Then, having been cast out of the prison, he was thrown into the sea, and was perfected. But those who had thrown him, being moved to repentance, came into the city, were baptized, and a great crowd with them. And after three days, his relics were cast up out of the sea; and his disciples, taking them, buried them.
On the same day.
The Contest of the Holy Hieromartyr Theopemptus and Theonas the martyr.
This man lived under the Emperor Diocletian. When a persecution was stirred up against the Christians, he was the first of all in that persecution to confess Christ, and he received the crown of the contest; for having been seized, he was thrown into a furnace that had been set on fire. And coming out of it healthy, he had one eye gouged out. And Theonas, being a magician, gave him a lethal poison to drink at the command of the emperor, so that he might die. And when he saw him suffering no harm, he believed in Christ. Then the emperor, blazing with anger, ordered the holy Theopemptus to be beheaded. As for Theonas, he cast him into a deep pit. And he ordered the executioners to pile earth upon him, and to trample it down. And this being done, he too was suffocated in the aforesaid place; and he surrendered his blessed soul to God; for whose sake both of the saints, with great eagerness, poured out their own blood, and renounced the world and things that are in the world.
On the same day.
The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Zosimus the monk and Athanasius the commentator, who suffered martyrdom in Cilicia.
The holy Zosimus was from Cilicia, dwelling in the wilderness together with the wild beasts. Having been seized by the prefect Domitianus, and compelled to deny Christ, and to sacrifice to *idols,*
and refusing to do so, he was slain by the sword in the year of the world 6314.
OF THE SAME MONTH. 9th Day.
89. The Contest of the Holy Martyr Polyeuctus.
In the reign of Decius and Valerian, this holy man flourished, being born in Melitene in Armenia, and serving as a soldier in that same region. When a cruel persecution was stirred up against the Christians, and those who refused to deny Christ were being slain daily, Saint Polyeuctus, having learned of this, openly and publicly professed Christ to be God. Furthermore, entering the temple of the idols, he shattered all the idols. Being caught, therefore, he was beaten by the command of the prefect. And since the prefect was his father-in-law, he exhorted him to abjure Christ and provide for his own safety. But he, calling upon his friend Nearchus, said: "I saw," he said, "in my sleep, my Christ coming and bringing me a precious robe. But remember me, my friend." His wife was present and encouraged him to remain constant in the faith of Christ. Therefore, giving thanks, he was beheaded.
On the same day.
90. Commemoration of the most holy martyr and confessor Theoctistus.
He is wanting.
On the same day.
91. Commemoration of the holy and glorious prophet Ahijah.
This holy and glorious prophet, Ahijah, was from Shiloh, from the city of Eli, where the tabernacle of the Jews had once been. He prophesied concerning Solomon, the prophet and king, that he would transgress and offend the Lord; and the event proved his words true. He also rebuked Jeroboam, because he walked with deceit before God. Furthermore, he saw in a vision a pair of oxen, which were trampling and walking over the people and persecuting the priests. He prophesied that it would come to pass that the people of the Jews would be trampled by the Gentiles, led into captivity, and consumed by famine, and that the priests would flee for fear and be vexed with many hardships. These things, and many others, he foretold, and died and was buried near the oak in Shiloh.
OF THE SAME MONTH. 10th Day.
92. Commemoration of Saint Gregory, Bishop of Nyssa.
This holy Gregory was the own brother of Basil the Great, Bishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia, and was distinguished for his wisdom and virtue. He was made bishop of Nyssa at the time when many heretics were attacking the orthodox. Therefore, the saint, sometimes by disputing openly from the testimonies of the holy Scriptures, and sometimes by writing, overcame them all and exterminated their heresy. When he took part in the second synod, which was held at Constantinople, he made use of such eloquence of speech that he stopped the mouths of the heretics and filled the orthodox with the greatest joy. Then to the church
to fill with joy. Having gone forth to his church and his flock, and having well nurtured both it and his pastors, and having written many God-inspired and soul-profiting books, in venerable gray hair and a good old age he departed to the Lord, rejoicing and exulting.
Eodem die
Commemoration of our holy Father Domitian, Bishop of Melitene.
Under the reign of Justin the Younger, this saint appeared, born of Christian, devout, and wealthy parents. He was taught by his parents the entire span of both secular learning and the Holy Scriptures. He also took a devout and faithful wife; but after she died shortly thereafter, he gave himself over entirely to the cultivation of piety and the fear of God. And [such] he became virtuous, insomuch that he even became Bishop of Melitene. They say that he attained such celebrity of name that he drew the King of the Persians to friendship, and to subjection to the Roman Emperor Maurice. For the King of the Persians wrote to the Roman [emperor], saying: "If you send Domitian, and he persuades me, I shall become not only your friend, but also your servant." And when this had come to pass, the whole city was joyful, and the whole world. Having entered into the city, the saint died; and his relics were transported to Melitene, performing many cures.
Eodem die
Commemoration of our holy Father Marcian, presbyter and oikonomos of the Great Church.
The ancestors of the blessed Marcian came to Constantinople from the great Rome. He flourished under the reigns of the most holy Marcian and the most pious Pulcheria; and being wealthy and devout, he built many great churches, having the Emperor also as his collaborator. For he built the church of Saint Irene, and that of Saint Anastasia in the Promontory of Domninos. This church he saved by his prayers even from a conflagration, when all things existing around it were burnt. He was, moreover, so merciful that once, while he was at leisure for divine prayers, when a poor man asked him [for alms] and he had nothing else at hand, he took off the cloak that he was wearing and gave it; he himself was left naked, with only his priestly stole. And he appeared to the clergy to be wearing a golden stole. Having performed many miracles and having raised a dead man to life, he fell asleep in peace.
Eodem die
Commemoration of the holy Dominica.
The holy Dominica lived under Theodosius the Great, and her life lasted until the reigns of Emperors Leo and Zeno. She was from the city of Carthage. But having left her own city and arrived at Constantinople, by a revelation she was received by Nectarius, the most holy Patriarch of Constantinople, who not only placed her in a monastery, which she herself [built] in honor of the holy pro—
prophet Zachariah, but also ordained her as deaconess. After this, she strove so earnestly in virtue that she received from God the gift of miracles; and being also enlarged by the prophetic spirit, she prophesied concerning emperors, patriarchs, and many others. Passing her life in this manner, and being held in the highest honor by emperors and patriarchs on account of her many virtues, she fell asleep in peace.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
The eleventh day.
 B 96 The Contest of the holy martyr Peter.
The holy martyr Peter was born in the village of Anea near Eleutheropolis, robust in body but more robust in faith in Christ; from his adolescence he manifested a wisdom beyond his years, when he taught everyone to withdraw from idols and approach Christ alone, the true God and Savior of men. When the governor of Eleutheropolis heard this about him, having sent officers, he had him arrested and compelled him to abjure Christ; but when he was unable to induce him to do this, he commanded that he be cast into a burning furnace of fire. Into this the saint was cast, and for many hours he gave thanks and prayed to God for the tranquility of the whole world and the holy Churches, and thus departed.
On the same day.
97 Commemoration of our holy Father Theodosius the Cenobiarch.
Our holy Father Theodosius, surnamed the Cenobiarch, was born in Cappadocia of pious parents, and having embraced the monastic life, he first went to Antioch to the holy Simeon the Stylite, and having received his blessing, he departed for Jerusalem. Thence he retired into the desert, where for a space of thirty years he consumed no other food besides dried figs and herbs. Then he performed many miracles and founded many monasteries: he put demons to flight by prayer; and when a great scarcity of grain occurred, and the monastery's granary had none, he found one single grain, and having blessed it, he filled the whole granary with wheat. On account of these, therefore, and many other prodigies, he became known to the emperors, and celebrated in the whole world for his virtues; at length he died in peace.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
The twelfth day.
98 The Contest of the holy martyr Tatiana.
The martyr Tatiana was from ancient Rome, during the reign of the emperor Alexander, the daughter of a rich and noble father, who had been prefect for the third time. Now, since she was a deaconess, she taught the people the faith of Christ and turned many away from idols. Wherefore, having been denounced to the emperor, and having been arrested by sent soldiers, she was brought before him: who [commanded] that she be taken with him to the temple of the idols...
and worship [them]. But the holy woman, having entered, by prayer broke the idols and threw them to the ground. Then she was struck upon the face, and her eyes were gouged out; she was hung up and scraped, and her head was shaven. Then she was delivered into the fire. And having come out unharmed, she fought against beasts; and having been delivered from the beasts, by the order of the emperor her head was severed.
On the same day: The Contest of the Holy Martyr Meortius.
He was a soldier during the reign of Diocletian, having performed bravely against the barbarians in wars, and was beloved both by the emperor and by his fellow soldiers. But being discovered to be a Christian, and being arrested and bound, he was brought before the emperor. And first he was stripped of his military armor and the belt of his office. Then he was bound by the four [limbs] and was mercilessly struck. For many hours as he was being struck, not even one sound escaped from him. The emperor, seeing him, how he endured the lashes and such great wounds, was astonished. And he said to the soldiers standing by, "What a soldier, who is dying so miserably!" Since no place was found on his entire body without a wound, he was released and cast into prison; where, singing psalms and praying, after eight days he died.
On the same day: The Contest of St. Athanasius the Confessor.

### [It is missing.]
In the Same Month: The Thirteenth: The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Hermylus and Stratonicus.
These saints flourished during the reign of the emperor Licinius. And the saint Hermylus was a deacon of the Church of Christ; and Licinius being stationed at the river Danube, and having heard about Saint Hermylus, seized him and punished him with iron rods. Then he shut him in prison, having ordered him to be guarded by the soldier Stratonicus. But an angel having come and comforted the holy Hermylus, Stratonicus saw [this] from the window of the prison, and believed in Christ for himself, and became a friend to Hermylus. Hermylus, having been led out of custody, was forced to worship the idols. And not being persuaded, he was struck with rods. Then with eagle-like claws they tore open his belly and his heart. Stratonicus, seeing this, wept; and being recognized, he was tortured. And both finally, being cast into the river Danube, were perfected.
In the Same Month: The Fourteenth: The Contest of our Holy Fathers who were slain on Mount Sinai.
These saints were from various places and lands.
Having abandoned the world, their relatives, and their wealth, they retreated to Mount Sinai and became monks, serving God alone and using wild herbs for food. Among their number was the holy Nilus, formerly the prefect of Constantinople, a wise and pious man who, having renounced worldly glory and riches for the sake of Christ, joined himself to the poor monks. But the envy of the devil stirred up against the holy Fathers the wild and ferocious race of the Blemmyes, who inhabit the coast from Arabia as far as the Red Sea of Egypt. These men, led by the hope of immense plunder, made an incursion upon the monks; but when they found nothing but the mats and hair-shirts of the Fathers, they turned to fury and slaughtered the innocent men. And in this manner did those saints depart.
On the Same Day
103 Commemoration of the holy Fathers who were slain on the same Mount Sinai by the Blemmyes in the time of the Emperor Diocletian.
These men were leading an ascetic life on Mount Sinai during the reign of the Emperor Diocletian, while Peter was archbishop of Alexandria. After the death of their leader, the Saracens issued forth from the uncultivated and desert places and slew many of the ascetics; but as the others took refuge in a fortification, a flame of fire was seen by the Saracens during the night, burning the whole mountain and reaching even to heaven itself; and it struck them with such terror that they threw down their arms and set themselves to flight. Those among the ascetics who were first slain were thirty-eight in number, bearing various wounds upon their bodies. From among these, two were found alive among the fallen, Sabas and Isaias. As for those who had perished, some were decapitated, others were flayed of their skin from one limb or another, and others were cut in half. These were all buried by those two monks who handed down this memory of them.
On the Same Day
104 Commemoration of the thirty-three holy Fathers who were slain in Raitho by the Blemmyes who dwelt there, where there were twelve fountains and seventy palm trees.
Three hundred Blemmyes crossed the Ethiopian Sea in large ships, and having reached the region of the Pharanites in a vessel they had chanced upon, they conquered the Pharanites who had encountered them, slaying one hundred and forty-seven men. Having abducted the women and children, they went to the fortress where the holy Fathers had a church. The saints barred the door and took refuge within the church, awaiting death. The barbarians entered, and finding no plunder there, they slew everyone and returned to the ship with the captive multitude. When they could not find it (for in the meantime those who had been pressed into service by the barbarians had sunk it and taken to flight), they were stirred to fury and slaughtered all the captives, and then were themselves slaughtered.
On the same day, the Commemoration of our holy Father Theodulus.
He was the son of Nilus the Wise, who had been prefect of Constantinople, but who resigned the glory of the world and departed to Mount Sinai, where he undertook the monastic life with his son. While both were dwelling in that place and living the ascetic life, Barbarians suddenly fell upon them and began to slaughter the holy Fathers; Nilus was able to escape, but his son Theodulus was captured along with another young man. They were bound and dragged away, and the Barbarians intended to slaughter the youths and sacrifice them to the morning star appearing before the sun. And one of them fled; but the Barbarians, having fallen asleep, and with the sun now risen and that star hidden, did not slaughter Theodulus. Wishing to sell him, they were asking for only two gold coins. And one of them drew his sword, intending to slaughter him. Then the bishop bought him and set him free; he spent the rest of his life in great holiness and fell asleep in the Lord.
On the same day, the Commemoration of our holy Father Stephen, the founder of the monastery of Chenolacus.
He was born in the East, being of noble birth and wealthy, but he renounced everything and became a monk. He went away to the desert of the Jordan, and passing through the monasteries of the great Fathers, he learned every virtue. During the reign of the Emperor Leo the Isaurian, he arrived in Constantinople and was welcomed by the most holy Patriarch Germanus, from whom he received the place where the monastery of Chenolacus now exists. Having founded the monastery, he gathered pious men for the glorification of God, and taught them every kind of virtue, instructing them how they might please God. He also taught those who came to him day by day, saying: "Brethren, do not forget God, knowing this, that this world is passing away and is nothing." Having taught these things and many others, and having shepherded his flock well, he surrendered his soul to God in peace.
On the same day, the Commemoration of Saint Athanasius.
Missing.
IN THE SAME MONTH.
THE FIFTEENTH.
The Falling Asleep of our holy Father Paul the Theban.
He flourished during the reign of the Emperor Decius, and was born in Thebes, a city of Egypt. He had a brother-in-law, and this brother-in-law intended to betray him to the governor as a Christian, so that he might inherit his share of the property; and learning of this beforehand, Paul fled and went away to the mountain of the desert. Proceeding little by little into the inner regions of the desert, he found a cave and entered it. And he clothed himself with garments made of palm leaves, which he stitched
together, and he sustained himself from their fruits. God, wishing to make his life manifest for the benefit of others, led the great Antony there, who supposed that no one else had entered more deeply into the desert than he. But upon finding him, he marveled. As they began to converse, a raven arrived carrying a whole loaf of bread, which it gave to them. When Antony returned to bring him a cloak, he found him already departed [to the Lord], and he glorified God.
On the same day: Commemoration of our holy Father John the Hut-dweller, who possessed the Golden Gospel.
John the Hut-dweller was born in Constantinople to Eutropius the patrician and Theodora. When he was a boy, he asked his parents, since his fellow students had Gospels to read and it was a shame for him not to have one, to procure that book for him as well. His parents therefore bought him a precious codex of the Gospels, adorned with gold, gems, and pearls, at the price of five hundred gold pieces. Having abandoned the school of learning, John went with a certain monk to the monastery of the Sleepless Ones, and having embraced the monastic life there, he fought his struggle most excellently. But the devil, having no way to attack him, injected into John a great desire to see his parents. Yet he did not yield to this thought; rather, he conquered and overcame the devil. For with the counsel of the abbot, he came to his house and saw his parents, but having built for himself a hut outside the gate, he remained there like a beggar until his death. Then, indeed, he was recognized by the golden book of the Gospels.
On the same day: The Contest of Saint Charitina.
Charitina the martyr was a slave of a certain Claudianus, a Christian and a pious man. In the times of Emperor Diocletian and the comes Domitianus, she was denounced for worshipping Christ, was arrested, and was presented to the comes. Having confessed Christ and professed herself a Christian, she was beaten most severely. Then, her head having been shaven, burning drops and very sharp vinegar were poured over her; soon her sides were burned with torches; finally, she was bound and cast into the sea. But miraculously saved, and emerging from the sea whole, she stood before the comes. The comes, seeing this, was astonished and ordered a wheel to be made, and having bound the saint to it, commanded that burning coals be placed under the wheel. When this had been done, Charitina was tormented. Then her teeth were pulled out, and, overwhelmed by most grievous pain and intolerable suffering, she surrendered her spirit to God.
IN THE SAME MONTH. The sixteenth day: The Adoration of the holy chain of the holy apostle Peter, by which, for the sake of Christ, he was bound by king Herod and cast into prison.
When king Herod had arrested James, the brother of the holy John the Theologian, and had beheaded him with a sword...
seeing that this was pleasing to the Jews, he apprehended also the holy Peter. And having bound him with two chains, he cast him into prison, wishing to put him to death. But an angel of God stood by him in the prison, and loosed him from his chains; and having opened the prison doors by night, he released him. And thus he escaped the hands of Herod. The chains, indeed, having been found by certain of the faithful and held in honor because of holy Peter, were kept by passing them down to posterity; and at last they were brought to Constantinople by pious emperors and deposited in the church of Saint Peter, which is near the church of Holy Sophia. And from that time unto this day they are honored, and venerated by the Christians in honor of the apostle Peter.
On the same day. The contest of the holy martyr Pansophius.
Pansophius the holy martyr was from Alexandria, the son of Nilus the proconsul. His father took care of him and taught him all manner of learning. But after the death of his father, he gave all his wealth to the poor, and departed into the desert; and he spent twenty-seven years in the desert praying to God. But certain wicked and envious men slandered him to the governor of Alexandria, the Augustalis, who had been sent by Decius to put the Christians to death. And the Augustalis sent and bound him and brought him before him; and he compelled him to deny Christ, and to worship the idols. And as he could not persuade him, he beat him most severely with rods. While being beaten for a long time, he gave thanks to God and prayed. Cursing the idols, he said that they were nothing. And in this manner, amidst stripes and tortures, he expired.
On the same day. The contest of the holy martyr Danax the lector.
The holy martyr Danax was from the country of Illyricum, from a place called Aulon. He was a lector of the holy Church, and he used to read the sacred books to the Christian people. Now in those days a gathering of certain rustic and infidel peoples invaded that region, and they raged against the Christians during those times. The holy Danax, therefore, having taken the sacred vessels of the church, fled to a certain fortified place five miles distant from Aulon, overlooking the sea. But they, having descended by ropes toward him, compelled him to sacrifice to Bacchus, saying that he was the author of wine. But he, confessing Christ, said that He is the creator of the world and of all things that are in the world; but that Bacchus is not a god, but a deaf and dumb idol. Wherefore, being enraged, they hacked him with the swords which they were holding, and cast him into the sea; and thus he had this end of his life.
OF THE SAME MONTH. The seventeenth day.
A 114 Commemoration of our holy Father Anthony the Great.
Anthony the Great, an Egyptian, was the greatest among ascetics. Born of wealthy parents, while still a young man he renounced everything, departed, and embraced the monastic life. Beaten by demons, he poured out prayers to God and was healed of his wounds. He overcame all the powers of the demons. Thus, he knew and crushed their stratagems as no one else did. And when he was assiduous in many fasts, sleeping on the ground, and prayers, he never relaxed his original zeal. Wherefore he merited the gift of prophecy and of miracles; he also discerned the souls of the just and of sinners, seeing the former taken up to God, and the latter cast into hell. He flourished in the times of Constantine the Great, and prophesied concerning the heresy of Arius. He had many disciples of his institution, and successors of his exercises, among whom were Athanasius the Great, Paul the Theban, and the great Hilarion. And having lived for ninety-five years, he died, and was buried by two of his disciples in an unknown place.
The same day.
115 The contest of the three holy youths Speusippus, Elasippus, and Belesippus, and their companions.
These three were triplet brothers, born from one and the same womb, Cappadocians by country, renowned horsemen and tamers of horses. When they had one day prepared a table to sacrifice to Zeus, and had invited their grandmother Neonilla, a Christian, to the feast with them, they heard her discoursing about Christ; and they accepted the faith of Christ, being taught that the gods of the gentiles are stones and wood, lacking soul, which bring certain destruction to men; and immediately rising up, they smashed the idols. When their masters, Palmatas, Hermogenes, and Codratus, learned of this, having first tried everything in vain to call them away from their purpose, they cast them into a huge burning furnace and consumed them. A certain woman, Junilla, holding her child, threw him in, professing herself a Christian; and immediately she was beheaded together with Neonilla, the grandmother of the martyrs. Finally, Turbo the notary, having run up and overturned the idols that had remained, professing himself a Christian, was beheaded.
OF THE SAME MONTH. The eighteenth day.
116 Commemoration of our holy Fathers Athanasius and Cyril.
Saint Athanasius was renowned in the time of the Emperor Constantine the Great. When he was archdeacon of the Church of Alexandria, he was sent by Archbishop Alexander to the Council of Nicaea, and shone in the synod, confounding Arius. After the death of Saint Alexander, he was elected patriarch of Alexandria in his place, and spent many
years suffering for the orthodox faith, enduring persecutions, fleeing, being sent into exile, and after struggling for Christ for the space of forty-two years, he finally fell asleep and was perfected. Saint Cyril, during the reign of Theodosius the Younger, was the nephew of Theophilus, patriarch of that same city of Alexandria, and furthermore became his successor. He also attended the synod convened in Ephesus for the deposition of the impious Nestorius, holding first place among the bishops. Having then shone in many other virtuous achievements, he departed to the Lord, receiving eternal life.
On the same day, a commemoration of the holy Eusebia, surnamed Xena.
The holy Eusebia, surnamed Xena, that is, "the Stranger," was from the city of Rome, noble, beautiful, and wealthy. When her parents wished to give her in marriage, and everything was prepared for the wedding, she secretly fled with two handmaids, boarded a ship, and sailed to Alexandria. Upon leaving the ship, she remained in a humble dwelling, uncertain because of her womanly weakness as to where she should go; but God sent to her a man who was a monk, by the name of Paul. He guided her toward salvation, for he took the women and led them to his own city, Mylassa near Caria. He built a church in the name of the holy protomartyr Stephen, and established them there. And the holy woman struggled so much that she was deemed worthy of many miracles; and having lived in this way, she ended her life.
On the same day, the contest of the holy martyr Theodula.
Theodula the martyr was from the city of Anazarbus during the reign of Diocletian. On account of her faith in Christ and her confession, she was arrested by Pelagius, the prefect of Anazarbus. She was beaten severely; yet, even while being tortured, she worked miracles. By her wonderworking she drew many to herself and persuaded them to approach Christ and become Christians. Among them were the Commentariensis and his assistant Helladius, who were both beheaded. The holy woman’s breasts were pierced with red-hot skewers and burned. Then she was hung by her hair from a cypress tree, and her feet were pierced with sharp spikes; and she remained hanging for a long time. Afterward, she was taken down and cast into a furnace that was burning intensely, together with other saints. There, praying for a long time, extending her hands, and giving thanks, she delivered her holy soul to God.
IN THE SAME MONTH.
The Nineteenth.
The contest of the holy martyr Theodotus, bishop of Cyrenia in Cyprus.
This man, during the reign of Licinius the emperor and the governorship of Sabinus of the island of Cyprus, was arrested for his confession of Christ and brought before the governor...
and was beaten severely with ox-hide scourges. Then he was hung upon a wooden frame, and his flesh was torn. Next, he was stretched out over a red-hot iron gridiron. But by a divine miracle, being preserved from the fire, he was compelled to run while shod with iron shoes into which sharp nails had been driven. Finally, he was cast into prison and remained in chains for as long as the persecution of the Christians lasted, until, by the edict of the Great Constantine then holding the Roman empire, he was released from prison. He occupied himself with the governance of his own church, teaching the word of our Lord Jesus Christ without any fear. And when he had lived for two years and had turned many toward piety, he departed unto the Lord and was honorably buried by his own devout disciples.
On the same day: The Contest of Saint Euphrasia the Martyr.
Saint Euphrasia the martyr was from the city of Nicomedia, during the reign of the emperor Maximian, being commended by the equal praise of her lineage and her character. Being arrested and compelled to sacrifice to idols, when she refused to do so, she was handed over to a barbarian and rustic man to be violated. She, fearing that force would be used against her, devised a ruse of this kind: "If you let me go," she said to him, "I will give you a potion which, if you carry it, neither sword nor any other thing will be able to harm you or inflict a wound; but you will be kept safe and unharmed from spears, swords, and missiles." He replied to her, "I would indeed like to learn this and be convinced of it." Then Euphrasia, immediately extending her neck, said, "Strike with your sword, for you will not be able to sever my head." He, having put faith in her words, struck her with the sword and severed her head from her neck. And thus she was snatched away from him.
On the same day: The Commemoration of our holy Fathers Macarius the Roman and the other Macarius of Egypt.
Saint Macarius the Roman, having been forced by his father against his will to take a wife, left her and decided to take flight and provide for his own salvation. Wherefore, an angel of God immediately took him and showed himself as his guide, leading him even to Paradise and placing him in a spot twenty miles distant from it; where three monks, Theophilus, Sergius, and Hyginus, found him, to whom he recounted the wondrous and stupendous things which he had seen. Finally, at a decrepit age, he departed unto the Lord. The other holy Macarius, tiny in stature, was from Alexandria in Egypt; who, having renounced the world and seeking the desert, received the monastic rule from a certain holy elder: and he so shone in virtues, by diligently observing the commandments of God, that he had power over demons, and saw them with his own eyes, and rebuked them. And having performed very many other miracles for the glory of Christ, he rested, rejoicing in peace.
JANUARY.
TWENTIETH DAY.
The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Valerian, Candidus, Aquila, and Eugenius.
These men contended under the Emperor Diocletian. For when the prefect Agricolaus and the Duke Lysias were executing the Christians—the forty holy martyrs at Sebaste, and the forty-five holy martyrs at Nicopolis—certain men came and said that some Christians were hiding in the mountains of Trapezus. Lysias sent men to seize them, and having bound them with ropes, he ordered them to be beaten most severely. Then, covering them with tallow and oil, he set fire to them and burned them. Then the holy Eugenius, having entered the temple of the idols with the prefect and having groaned greatly to God and prayed, broke the idols into pieces. And at that time many believed in Christ. After this, the holy Candidus, Aquila, and Valerian were beheaded; but the holy Eugenius was stretched upon the rack, cast into prison, tortured with various torments, and thrown into fire. Having emerged from it unharmed, he was eventually beheaded as well.
On the same day.
The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Bassus, Eusebius, Eutychius, and Basilides.
These saints also suffered martyrdom during the time of the impious Emperor Diocletian. Being among the first in the palace and distinguished by wealth and glory, when they saw the holy bishop Theopemptus martyred and tortured, they believed in Christ. Loosening their belts, they threw them down before the emperor, declaring themselves Christians. Then the emperor commanded that, if they spoke truly, they should go and give all their money to others, which they did with great readiness. A pit having been dug, the holy Bassus was lowered into it up to his chest, and his hands having been cut off, he gave up his spirit to the Lord. The holy Eusebius, suspended head-downward between two columns, was cut in two with an axe and died. Eutychius, having had his hands and feet tied to four stakes, was torn into three parts and thus fell asleep; and the holy Basilides was cut through the middle of his belly with a sword and departed into the presence of the Lord.
On the same day.
The Contest of the Holy Martyrs Innas, Rimmas, and Pinnas.
These saints were from Scythia, from the northern region, and were disciples of the holy Apostle Andrew. Teaching in the name of Christ and turning many of the Barbarians to the true faith, they baptized them. For this reason, they were seized by the prince of the Barbarians, and though many attempts were made to force them to renounce Christ and sacrifice to idols, they were not persuaded. And when a very severe winter occurred, and the rivers were frozen by the cold, so that it was possible to walk not only for men but also for horses and wagons upon the ice…
they planted large pieces of wood there, as if they were trees rooted in the ground, and bound the saints there. And as the water tossed and the ice gradually increased, reaching up to the necks of the holy ones, overwhelmed by the most acute pain of the cold, they surrendered their blessed souls to the Lord.
Eodem die
Commemoration of our holy Father Euthymius, priest and master of the desert.
Euthymius the Great, under the emperor Gratian, was born at Melitene in Armenia, to Paul and Dionysia. Since they were barren, they learned from God that they would have a son, upon whose birth the whole world and all the Churches would be of good cheer; wherefore, when the boy was born, they called him Euthymius, which means "in good and cheerful spirits." And when he grew up, he was first ordained a cleric by the bishop of that place; then a presbyter, and exarch of the monasteries. Soon he traveled to Jerusalem and withdrew into the cave of the holy Theoctistus, and lived there a quiet life; and he so progressed in the practice of virtues that, by his reputation for supreme holiness, he drew Saracens every day to the faith of Christ, and baptized them along with their families. He was also famous for many miracles: and when he was once celebrating the liturgy, he was seen to have taken on the appearance of a pillar of fire. Finally, in the ninety-seventh year of his age, in a good old age, he migrated to the Lord, in the reign of Leo the Great.
EODEM MENSE.
Die vicesima prima
Contest of the holy martyr Neophytus
Neophytus the martyr of Christ was from the city of Nicaea in Bithynia, born under the emperor Diocletian to Christian parents, Theodore and Florentia. While yet a boy, he was full of the grace of God and performed miracles. For when he went to school with his classmates, he would pray and nourish them. Wherever he went, a dove followed him, and conversed with him in a human voice; when his mother saw it conversing with the boy, she fainted from fear and fell down. But the saint recalled her to life from death by his prayers. He frequently ascended Mount Olympus, and devoted himself to prayer there. Wherefore, having been reported to the governor Decius, and having been seized by his order, he was beaten most cruelly with scourges; soon after, he was cast into a burning furnace, but escaped from it unharmed. He was then condemned to the beasts; and when he remained unhurt by them, he was at length slain by the sword by one of those who stood by the governor.
Eodem die
Commemoration of Saint Zosimus, Bishop of Syracuse.
Our holy Father Zosimus was born in Sicily to faithful, pious, and wealthy parents, who had an estate near the monastery of the holy virgin Lucia. When he had been brought into the light,
he was offered by them to Saint Lucia and educated in that same monastery, becoming the sacristan of her precious shrine. If ever he went to visit his parents, he was not permitted to tarry with them for any long time, but was immediately sent back to the church of the Saint, as his parents told him that he must be where he had been dedicated by them. When he had spent thirty years in the monastery, he was made its superior. Afterwards, having been ordained Bishop of the city of Syracuse by Theodore, the Pope of Rome, when he became bishop he did not neglect the people, but by teaching many about eternal life, and about judgment and retribution, and by governing the Church entrusted to him excellently, he ended his life, having attained fifty years of age.
IN THE SAME MONTH. THE TWENTY-SECOND DAY.
The struggle of the holy apostle Timothy, Bishop of Ephesus, disciple of the holy apostle Paul.
Timothy, the apostle of Christ, was born at the city of Lystra, of a Greek father and a Jewish mother. He became a disciple of the holy apostle Paul and a companion of his preaching, and he was ordained by the same holy Paul as the first bishop of Ephesus during the reign of Emperor Nero, before Saint John the Theologian. For the Theologian came to Ephesus later, after the falling asleep of the holy Theotokos. Now Saint Timothy, while instructing the people in the faith of Christ, once seeing the Greeks celebrating an abominable feast day to their idols, where men and women were shamelessly committing base acts together, ran to them and exhorted them to desist from this wickedness. So far from succeeding in anything, he was instead killed by them with the staves which they held in their hands, at the very entrance of the city, where his precious relics were also buried.
On the same day.
The struggle of the holy martyr Vincent, deacon.
This man was from the city of Augusta, under the reign of Emperor Diocletian and Domitian the governor. He was arrested with Bishop Valerius and brought to the city called Valentia and delivered to prison. Brought forth from there, he was presented to Domitian; he was beaten with rods and mangled with iron claws, and held suspended for a long time on the rack; soon he was scorched on his sides with torches of fire, and pierced with red-hot iron spits. When the executioners were exhausted, and the holy martyr stood sound and uninjured by the grace of God, the tyrant changed his plan and began to cajole him, saying, "Behold, I will set you free, and I will not put you to death, if only you will consent to deny your Christ and worship the gods." But the holy man, not even bearing to hear his blasphemies, delivered his spirit to God in prayer; and his precious relics were buried in that same place by faithful and pious men.
The Same Day: The Contest of the Holy Martyr Anastasius the Persian
He was from Persia, under the reign of the Romans of Heraclius, and of the Persians of Chosroes, son of Maguntinus. Being himself a magus, he was enrolled in the ranks of the young soldiers. When the Persians made an incursion into the holy places and, along with the other spoils, carried off the precious wood of the Lord’s Cross, they spread abroad among the populace that the God of the Christians had come under their dominion; for they considered the precious Cross to be God, because of the miracles performed by it. Anastasius, moved by these reports and having carefully considered the matter, learned the whole dispensation of Christ and embraced His faith. And he came first with the Persians to Chalcedon; and there, being more fully imbued with the fear of God, he renounced his military service and the world, and went to Hierapolis, and from there to Jerusalem; and he was baptized, and became a monk in the laura of St. Sabas. Later, burning with the desire to bear witness for Christ through martyrdom, he returned to his own country; and being seized by the Persians and tortured with many torments, he was beheaded.
The Same Day: The Translation of the Precious Relics of the Same Holy Martyr Anastasius the Persian
When Emperor Heraclius had moved into Persia and King Chosroes had been slain, a certain monk from the monastery of the Martyr, seeing the emperor’s army, rejoiced and followed them as though they were Christians. When they returned, he also went back to his own Abbot and reported to him that he had brought the *colobium* of the martyr Anastasius, and told him that a man in Persia oppressed by a demon had been set free by being clothed in that *colobium*. And Heraclius, in the twentieth year of his reign, restored the precious wood of the Cross to Jerusalem. A certain bishop, having been sent by the catholic archbishop to the Roman regions, took the relics of the saint and brought them to Caesarea; and having left a small portion there, he kept the rest with himself. The precious head of the martyr, and his icon, are venerated by the faithful in great and ancient Rome.
The Same Day: The Contest of the Holy Manuel, George, Leo, and their Companions, who were Killed by the Impious Bulgarians under the Reign of Leo the Armenian
During the reign of Leo the Armenian, Crumus, the prince of the Bulgarians, with a great multitude of people, came to Adrianople in Thrace; and having taken it by the right of war, he also seized the most holy Bishop Manuel. He first cut off his hands from his shoulders, and then, having cut him in two with a sword, he delivered him to the wild beasts to be devoured. For this impiety, he was struck with blindness and became hated by his own people, and was strangled by them with ropes. Tzocus, the most impious, succeeded to the principality of the Bulgarians and [brought] all the captive Christians—dukes, priests, deacons, and laity—into
a forced denial of the Christian faith; for the Bulgarians were not yet imbued with the faith of Christ. And since they would not do this, he ordered some to be beheaded, and others he killed miserably after they had been tortured with various torments.
IN THE SAME MONTH, THE TWENTY-THIRD DAY
The Contest of the Holy and Much-Suffering Clement, Bishop of Ancyra in the Province of Galatia
During the reigns of Diocletian and Maximian, the holy Clement was from Ancyra in Galatia, born of a Gentile father and a Christian mother named Euphrosyne, who also prophesied concerning her son, that he was destined to suffer martyrdom for Christ. After his mother’s death, he was raised by a wealthy woman named Sophia. He became a monk, then a deacon, then a priest, and after this a bishop, at the age of twenty. While he was baptizing the children of the Greeks, he was seized by the Vicar Domitian and punished in Ancyra; then in Rome by Diocletian; then in Nicomedia by Maximian and Agrippinus; and again in Ancyra; then in Amisus; then in Tarsus; and back again to Ancyra, where, having been cast into prison and officiating there at the liturgy, he was put to the sword along with the deacons Christophoros and Chariton by the prefect Alexander.
On the same day
The Contest of the holy martyr Agathangelos and the men, women, and children who suffered martyrdom with him
While the holy Clement was in custody in Rome with many others, Agathangelos, who was among their number, saw an angel sent by God to the holy Clement, who presented him with bread and wine and then departed. Moved by this, he accepted the faith of Christ along with many others. When Diocletian learned of this, he had them all beheaded. But the holy Agathangelos, though he could have fled, yet when he learned that the holy Clement was being taken to Nicomedia by ship, he preceded him, and boarding the vessel, he waited for him. Seeing him, the holy Clement rejoiced, and thought him the presence of a good angel. Agathangelos therefore followed him until he reached Ancyra, to Lucius. And there he was beheaded by Lucius along with the children, men, and women who had believed in Christ along with him and the holy Clement.
IN THE SAME MONTH, THE TWENTY-FOURTH DAY
The Contest of the holy martyrs Paul, Pausirius, and Theodotion
These men lived under the reign of Diocletian and the prefect Arrianus in the city of Cleopatris; they were brothers by birth, and having renounced the world, they embraced the monastic life. Afterward, being seized by the prefect, they were subjected to torments. They were of the age when they were seized...
Paul was thirty-seven years old, and Pausirius twenty-five. But Theodotion, having abandoned the monastic life, had addicted himself to robbery. When he heard that his brothers had been arrested, he came with the desire to see them; but when he saw them being tormented, he did not dare to approach closely. Instead, from a high place, looking on from a distance, he pondered within himself what kind of inheritance his brothers were about to receive. Running forward, therefore, and professing himself a Christian, he seized the prefect with his hand and threw him from the tribunal. For this reason, he was immediately beheaded. But Paul and Pausirius were cast into the river and thus attained their consummation.
ON THE SAME MONTH. The twenty-fifth day.
B 
Commemoration of our holy Father Gregory the Theologian.
Gregory the Great, the Theologian, was born in the region of Cappadocia, in the city of Nazianzus, to Gregory and Nonna, and flourished during the reign of Constantius, the son of Constantine the Great; he lived until the time of Theodosius the Great. Born through a revelation and reared, for the sake of his studies, he journeyed first to Caesarea, then to Alexandria, and finally to Athens. While he was immersed in the sciences, the great Basil also arrived, and both of them labored together to acquire virtue and learning. Afterward, they returned to their own homeland. Gregory indeed became bishop of Sasima; then, because his father was already burdened by old age, he diligently fulfilled his duty of both teaching and celebrating the liturgy in his stead in Nazianzus. After this, on account of the Arian heresy, he went to Byzantium, confirmed the orthodox with his sermons, and was even proclaimed patriarch of Constantinople. But shortly thereafter, having abdicated the patriarchate, he retired to his own estate and finished his life in most honorable leisure.
ON THE SAME MONTH. The twenty-sixth day.
Commemoration of the terrible, great, and wondrous earthquake.
In the final times of the Emperor Theodosius the Younger, son of Arcadius and Eudoxia, in the month of January, on the twenty-sixth day, a horrible earthquake occurred in Constantinople—such as had never happened since the founding of the city until that day—so that both the walls and the greatest part of the city collapsed. By the same earthquake, other cities of Thrace, Macedonia, and Bithynia were also shaken, and it lasted for three months. For this reason, the Emperor Theodosius, with the patriarch, the clergy, and the entire populace, walking barefoot and pouring forth a flood of tears, having instituted a public supplication, said to God: "Deliver us, O Lord, from Thy righteous anger and from all our sins, through repentance; for Thou hast moved the earth and troubled it on account of our sins, casting fear into our hearts, that we may glorify Thee, who alone art kind and merciful." Thenceforth, therefore, the memory of this earthquake is celebrated.
On the same day.
Commemoration of the holy Xenophon, and his wife, and their children Arcadius and John.
Our holy Father Xenophon was a senator in Constantinople, pious, merciful, and righteous, and possessed of every virtue. And being glorious and very rich, he begat two sons, Arcadius and John, and having brought them up in the instruction and admonition of the Lord, he sent them to the city of Berytus, so that they might study the laws. For that city had at that time libraries of all kinds of learning. But when his sons suffered shipwreck, and the father learned this from one of his servants, he set out with his wife to search for them. And having found them in Jerusalem, cast up from the sea and having become monks, and recognizing them, he himself and his wife also put on the monastic habit. And they progressed so far in virtue as to be accounted worthy to perform miracles; and in this way they passed away in the Lord, together with their sons.
ON THE SAME MONTH. 27th.
The contest of the holy martyrs Ananias the presbyter, Peter the jailer, and seven other soldiers.
These flourished under the Emperor Diocletian and the prefect Maximus. When the holy Ananias approached this Maximus and confessed himself to be a Christian, and reviled the idols, he was stripped and beaten severely with rods; and his sides were scored with red-hot irons, and rubbed with vinegar, salt, and haircloth. Then he prayed, and the temple and the idols fell down. Then he was cast into prison, and he remained without food for seven days; and he was fed by Christ with heavenly food. When Peter the jailer saw this, he fell at his feet and was baptized. And when one prefect had succeeded another, Maximus by name, he bound the holy men to a wheel and placed them upon a heated gridiron. But the seven soldiers who were torturing the saints saw the fire cooling, and they accepted the faith of Christ; and having been thrown into the sea along with them by the executioners, they finished their lives.
On the same day.
The translation of the relics of our holy Father John Chrysostom.
Saint John Chrysostom, having been sent into exile, went away to Comana in Armenia, where the relics of the holy Basiliscus lie, who also appeared to him, saying: "Do not fear, brother John; for tomorrow we shall be together." The same saint appeared to the sexton, saying: "Prepare a place for the great martyr John, who is coming." Therefore John, having put on a white stole and having celebrated the liturgy, and having spoken the words which he always had in his mouth: "Glory to God for all things," he gave up his spirit, and was laid near the tomb of the holy martyr Basiliscus. But thirty-three years later, through the agency of Proclus the patriarch,
Theodosius the emperor, wishing to bring back the relics, sent a message; but since the sarcophagus could not be opened, the emperor, struck with grief, wrote a letter to the saint in which he besought him to come; he was immediately heard. Therefore, the precious relics were translated to Constantinople and were honorably interred in the church of the Holy Apostles.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
The twenty-eighth day.
Commemoration of our holy Father Ephrem the Syrian.
He was from the East, a Syrian by race, and a citizen of Edessa, born of Christian parents; he lived from the times of Constantine the Great until the reign of Valens. While he was still an infant, his parents saw in a dream a vine proceeding from his tongue, which filled the whole earth, and the birds of the sky came and plucked from its fruit. Now the vine signified the knowledge that he was to possess. He excelled in virtues to such a degree that he was profitable to many. When he came to Basil the Great in the city of Caesarea, he was ordained a presbyter by him, and at the same time he began to speak Greek, although he had previously spoken only Syriac. Having later composed books useful for the soul, and having taught and benefited many, he migrated to God in deep old age, giving thanks.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
The twenty-ninth day.
Translation of the relics of the holy martyr Ignatius, patriarch of Antioch.
Blessed Ignatius the God-bearer was sent by the emperor Trajan from Antioch to Rome on account of his confession of Christ; he fought with beasts and was suffocated by lions, while the whole senate stood in the theater and watched: this was what the saint had obtained from God through prayer; for he said: "Even if it be necessary for me to be consumed by beasts, yet I will not deny my Christ; but I desire to be ground by the teeth of the beasts, that I may be found as pure bread for my God." When, therefore, he was suffocated by the lions having been let loose upon him, his relics were raised up by the Christians, and with pomp and honor were translated from Rome to Antioch, with the whole city coming out to meet them with candles, psalms, hymns, and spiritual songs; they were interred in that same city in a most honorable and magnificent sepulcher for its protection and safeguard.
On the same day.
The contest of the holy martyrs Sarbelus the priest, and Bebaia his sister.
These flourished under the emperor Trajan. Now the holy priest Sarbelus was a priest of idols: and when a feast day was being celebrated for the demons, he was performing impure sacrifices according to custom. But having been rebuked by Barsimaeus, the bishop of Edessa, and reproached for being the author of the ruin and destruction of many; led by divine favor to repentance, and having [applied] the bishop’s word—
he was baptized by the bishop, and having witnessed for Christ, together with his sister Babæa. The bishop, having learned this, presented himself to the governor Lysias; and he was beaten severely. Then, as the persecution ceased for a while, he went out of the prison; and having returned to the church, and having lived according to all virtue as was his custom in service to God, he reached his end, having received eternal life.
On the same day
The Contest of Saint Theophilus the Younger
This man was from Constantinople, under the reign of Constantine and Irene, the orthodox emperors; he was a man of the senatorial order, and had been appointed by the emperors as a commander of the army—
sent to the Cibyrrhaeote theme, when the Saracen fleet appeared, he drew up for battle. Now he had as comrades in the forces three other commanders. And when he himself, as the first of them with his own dromon, having made an attack upon the Barbarians, fought most bravely, the other commanders, envying his glory, took to flight and left him alone. Surrounded thus, the Barbarians captured him; and returning home, they shut him up in prison: from which, being brought out four years later, when he was urged to abjure the Christian faith and accept the Hagarene superstition, and could not be induced to do so, he ended his life having been beheaded.
ON THE SAME MONTH.
The thirty-first day.
Contest of the holy wonder-workers Cyrus and John
These flourished under the emperor Diocletian. Now, the holy Cyrus was by country an Alexandrian, and by profession a physician; and since he practiced his art without any fee, he simultaneously taught the faith in Christ. Wherefore, being denounced to the prefect of the city, he was struck with fear, and fled into Arabia; and having embraced the monastic life, he dwelt in a place near the sea. But the holy John, having departed for Jerusalem, and having been made certain of the miracles of the holy Cyrus, went to him and joined his manner of life with him. But when a certain Christian woman, by name Athanasia, and her three daughters, Theodota, Theoctista, and Eudoxia, were seized by the prefect to be tortured, they, fearing lest she, as a woman, might fail in the torments, approached and encouraged her. For this reason, being seized, after many tortures they were beheaded.
On the same day.
Contest of the holy martyr Victorinus, and the six with him
Under the emperor Decius, these existed from the city of Corinth. Being seized, however, and delivered to the proconsul Tertius, they were tortured severely. For Victorinus, having had his right eye gouged out, and his hands and feet cut off, was crushed in a large marble mortar and died. Victor [read: Victor, in the Latin, but note the text variation], his tongue cut out, was crushed in the same manner in the mortar and finished his course. Nicephorus was pressed until blood gushed from his nostrils; then, having been hung by the hair, and finally crushed in the same mortar, he breathed out his soul. Claudianus, with hands and feet mutilated, and having been hung up, departed this life. Diodorus was consumed by fire. Sarapion, hung head downward, was beheaded. Papias, finally, having had his hands and feet amputated, and having been tied to a stone, was cast into the depths.
On the same day.
Contest of the holy martyr Tryphaena
Tryphaena, the martyr of Christ, was from the city of Cyzicus. She was the daughter of Anastasius, a patrician but a Gentile, and of Socratia, a Christian, and was herself a Christian. But when the festival
was being celebrated to the idols, moved by divine zeal, she rushed into the midst of the people, spat upon the abominable sacred rites of the Gentiles, and heaped insults upon those who were watching them, teaching that they must renounce idols, turn to Christ, and receive baptism. For this reason, having been seized by the order of the prefect Caesarius, she was thrown into a furnace. Having been delivered from the furnace, she was hung upon a piece of wood, with very sharp iron nails fixed into the ground below. And being released from the wood, she fell upon the nails and was afflicted with most severe pain. Then various wild beasts were sent against her; when the others did not dare to approach her, a bull ran up and, having driven its horns into her vitals, tore her in two; and she immediately gave up her spirit, and thus her body was buried by the faithful.
MENSE FEBRUARIO.
Die prima.
Certamen sancti martyris Tryphonis.
Tryphon the martyr was from the region of Phrygia, during the reign of Gordian. While still a boy, he herded geese; yet at the same time he was diligent in acquiring virtues and scrupulously fulfilled the commandments of God: whence he received the gift of healings, so that there was no kind of disease that he could not cure. He even healed the emperor's daughter, who was oppressed by a demon, and he compelled the demon himself, appearing in the form of an extremely black dog, to reveal his own deceptions and deceits; this affair brought many to the faith of Christ. After these things, under the emperor Decius, he was arrested by the prefect Aquilinus and led in chains to Nicaea; there, tied to wild horses, he was dragged through impassable places in the winter season: then, naked, he was rolled upon sharp nails: finally, scorched about the sides with torches, he was beheaded.
Eodem die.
Commemoratio sancti Patris nostri Bendimiani, discipuli sancti Auxentii.
